7:["$","p",null,{"children":["Quantum error mitigation (QEM) encompasses algorithmic protocols, such as zero-noise extrapolation (ZNE) ",["$","$L2",null,{"href":"#references","children":"[2]"}]," and probabilistic error cancellation (PEC) ",["$","$L2",null,{"href":"#references","children":"[3]"}],", that are designed to suppress noise-induced bias in expectation values on noisy intermediate-scale quantum (NISQ) hardware, without the large qubit overhead of full fault tolerance. These methods are critical for extracting reliable physical simulations and benchmark results from current processors. Although many QEM methods have been developed in recent years, almost all of them share a fundamental trade-off: they rely on significantly more QPU time to achieve accuracy. Their defining limitation is an exponential sampling overhead, because reconstructing an unbiased signal from noisy circuits greatly increases the statistical variance of the estimator. Consequently, overcoming higher noise levels requires orders of magnitude more measurement shots, which leads to significantly longer QPU runtimes and substantial hardware bottlenecks ",["$","$L2",null,{"href":"#references","children":"[4, 5, 6]"}],"."],"className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]

9:["$","p",null,{"children":["With AI-QEM, you can run quantum circuits on noisy QPUs and obtain improved expectation values compared to other AI-based error mitigation techniques, without any additional QPU runtime overhead. The approach uses a deep neural network with a graph convolutional network (GCN)-Transformer architecture. The network is trained on features that include circuit structure, backend topology, Pauli observables, and qubit calibrations, and it predicts a correction factor, denoted by ",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-mathml","children":["$","math",null,{"xmlns":"http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML","children":["$","semantics",null,{"children":[["$","mrow",null,{"children":["$","mi",null,{"children":"ζ"}]}],["$","annotation",null,{"encoding":"application/x-tex","children":"\\zeta"}]]}]}]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-html","aria-hidden":"true","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"base","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"strut","style":{"height":"0.8889em","verticalAlign":"-0.1944em"}}],["$","span",null,{"className":"mord mathnormal","style":{"marginRight":"0.07378em"},"children":"ζ"}]]}]}]]}],". Experiments show that AI-QEM produces better results than other AI-based mitigation methods, particularly for circuits with large depth (gate layers) and width (number of qubits), which makes it well suited for utility-scale quantum experiments. The table in the ",["$","$L2",null,{"href":"#benchmarks","children":"Benchmarks"}]," section shows that the GCN-Transformer-based model surpasses random forest (RF)-based models in the 80- to 150-qubit range, with a mean absolute error (MAE) of 0.343. The MAE is the average, over a large number of circuit and observable configurations, of the absolute difference between the ideal expectation value and the expectation value after the model mitigates the noisy hardware result. 12:["$","p",null,{"children":"The AI-QEM technique begins with dataset construction, in which random quantum circuits are generated with various parameter settings. For each circuit, both the ideal expectation values (obtained through simulation) and the noisy expectation values (obtained from hardware execution) are computed. These results, along with the corresponding quantum circuits and backend information, are stored in a dataset repository. The dataset is then used to train a machine-learning or deep-learning model that can mitigate noise in quantum computations, and the trained model is saved for future use. During inference, the trained model takes as input a quantum circuit, its noisy expectation value computed on hardware, the relevant observables, and backend information. The model then processes these inputs to produce a noise-mitigated expectation value.","className":"group paragraph-el text-text-primary mx-0"}]
